     * [254]v
     * [255]t
     * [256]e

   In [257]mathematics, the limit of a function is a fundamental concept
   in [258]calculus and [259]analysis concerning the behavior of that
   [260]function near a particular [261]input which may or may not be in
   the [262]domain of the function.

   Formal definitions, first devised in the early 19th century, are given
   below. Informally, a function f assigns an [263]output f(x) to every
   input x. We say that the function has a limit L at an input p, if f(x)
   gets closer and closer to L as x moves closer and closer to p. More
   specifically, the output value can be made arbitrarily close to L if
   the input to f is taken sufficiently close to p. On the other hand, if
   some inputs very close to p are taken to outputs that stay a fixed
   distance apart, then we say the limit does not exist.

   The notion of a limit has many applications in [264]modern calculus. In
   particular, the many definitions of [265]continuity employ the concept
   of limit: roughly, a function is continuous if all of its limits agree
   with the values of the function. The concept of limit also appears in
   the definition of the [266]derivative: in the calculus of one variable,
   this is the limiting value of the [267]slope of [268]secant lines to
   the graph of a function.

History

   [[269]edit]

   Although implicit in the [270]development of calculus of the 17th and
   18th centuries, the modern idea of the limit of a function goes back to
   [271]Bolzano who, in 1817, introduced the basics of the epsilon-delta
   technique (see [272](ε, δ)-definition of limit below) to define
   continuous functions. However, his work was not known during his
   lifetime.^[273][1]

   In his 1821 book [274]Cours d'analyse, [275]Augustin-Louis Cauchy
   discussed variable quantities, [276]infinitesimals and limits, and
   defined continuity of
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>y</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mi>f</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle y=f(x)}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle y=f(x)} by saying that an infinitesimal change in x
   necessarily produces an infinitesimal change in y, while Grabiner
   claims that he used a rigorous epsilon-delta definition in
   proofs.^[277][2] In 1861, [278]Weierstrass first introduced the
   epsilon-delta definition of limit in the form it is usually written
   today.^[279][3] He also introduced the notations
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\textstyle \lim }</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\textstyle \lim } and
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"> <mstyle displaystyle="false"
   scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msub> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mn>0</mn> </mrow> </msub> </mrow> </munder>
   <mstyle displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle>
   </mstyle> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\textstyle \textstyle \lim _{x\to
   x_{0}}\displaystyle .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\textstyle \textstyle \lim _{x\to x_{0}}\displaystyle .} ^[280][4]

   The modern notation of placing the arrow below the limit symbol is due
   to [281]Hardy, which is introduced in his book [282]A Course of Pure
   Mathematics in 1908.^[283][5]

Motivation

   [[284]edit]

   Imagine a person walking on a landscape represented by the graph y =
   f(x). Their horizontal position is given by x, much like the position
   given by a map of the land or by a [285]global positioning system.
   Their altitude is given by the coordinate y. Suppose they walk towards
   a position x = p, as they get closer and closer to this point, they
   will notice that their altitude approaches a specific value L. If asked
   about the altitude corresponding to x = p, they would reply by saying y
   = L.

   What, then, does it mean to say, their altitude is approaching L? It
   means that their altitude gets nearer and nearer to L—except for a
   possible small error in accuracy. For example, suppose we set a
   particular accuracy goal for our traveler: they must get within ten
   meters of L. They report back that indeed, they can get within ten
   vertical meters of L, arguing that as long as they are within fifty
   horizontal meters of p, their altitude is always within ten meters of
   L.

   The accuracy goal is then changed: can they get within one vertical
   meter? Yes, supposing that they are able to move within five horizontal
   meters of p, their altitude will always remain within one meter from
   the target altitude L. Summarizing the aforementioned concept we can
   say that the traveler's altitude approaches L as their horizontal
   position approaches p, so as to say that for every target accuracy
   goal, however small it may be, there is some neighbourhood of p where
   all (not just some) altitudes correspond to all the horizontal
   positions, except maybe the horizontal position p itself, in that
   neighbourhood fulfill that accuracy goal.

   The initial informal statement can now be explicated:

   The limit of a function f(x) as x approaches p is a number L with the
   following property: given any target distance from L, there is a
   distance from p within which the values of f(x) remain within the
   target distance.

   In fact, this explicit statement is quite close to the formal
   definition of the limit of a function, with values in a
   [286]topological space.

   More specifically, to say that

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>p</mi> </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   p}f(x)=L,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p}f(x)=L,}

   is to say that f(x) can be made as close to L as desired, by making x
   close enough, but not equal, to p.

   The following definitions, known as (ε, δ)-definitions, are the
   generally accepted definitions for the limit of a function in various
   contexts.

Functions of a single variable

   [[287]edit]

(ε, δ)-definition of limit

   [[288]edit]

   [289][220px-Epsilon-delta_limit.svg.png] For the depicted f, a, and b,
   we can ensure that the value f(x) is within an arbitrarily small
   interval (b – ε, b + ε) by restricting x to a sufficiently small
   interval (a – δ, a + δ). Hence f(x) → b as x → a.

   Suppose
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:\mathbb {R}
   \rightarrow \mathbb {R} }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:\mathbb {R} \rightarrow \mathbb {R} } is a function
   defined on the [290]real line, and there are two real numbers p and L.
   One would say that the limit of f, as x approaches p, is L and
   written^[291][6]

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>p</mi> </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   p}f(x)=L,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p}f(x)=L,}

   or alternatively, say f(x) tends to L as x tends to p, and written:

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mtext> as </mtext> </mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">→</mo>
   <mi>p</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f(x)\to L{\text{ as }}x\to
   p,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)\to L{\text{ as }}x\to p,}

   if the following property holds: for every real ε > 0, there exists a
   real δ > 0 such that for all real x, 0 < |x − p| < δ implies |f(x) − L|
   < ε.^[292][6] Symbolically,
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo><</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in \mathbb {R}
   )\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall
   x\in \mathbb {R} )\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   For example, we may say
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mn>2</mn> </mrow> </munder> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>4</mn> <mi>x</mi> <mo>+</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mn>9</mn> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   2}(4x+1)=9}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 2}(4x+1)=9} because for every real ε > 0, we
   can take δ = ε/4, so that for all real x, if 0 < |x − 2| < δ, then |4x
   + 1 − 9| < ε.

   A more general definition applies for functions defined on [293]subsets
   of the real line. Let S be a subset of ⁠
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \mathbb {R}
   .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \mathbb {R} .} ⁠ Let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb
   {R} }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb {R} } be a [294]real-valued function. Let
   p be a point such that there exists some open interval (a, b)
   containing p with
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>a</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>∪</mo>
   <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>⊂</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   (a,p)\cup (p,b)\subset S.}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (a,p)\cup (p,b)\subset S.} It is then said that the
   limit of f as x approaches p is L, if:

   For every real ε > 0, there exists a real δ > 0 such that for all x ∈
   (a, b), 0 < |x − p| < δ implies that |f(x) − L| < ε.

   Or, symbolically:
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>a</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn>
   <mo><</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo>
   </mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in
   (a,b))\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall
   x\in (a,b))\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   For example, we may say
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </munder> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <msqrt> <mi>x</mi> <mo>+</mo> <mn>3</mn>
   </msqrt> </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mn>2</mn> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 1}{\sqrt
   {x+3}}=2}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 1}{\sqrt {x+3}}=2} because for every real ε
   > 0, we can take δ = ε, so that for all real x ≥ −3, if 0 < |x − 1| <
   δ, then |f(x) − 2| < ε. In this example, S = [−3, ∞) contains open
   intervals around the point 1 (for example, the interval (0, 2)).

   Here, note that the value of the limit does not depend on f being
   defined at p, nor on the value f(p)—if it is defined. For example, let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>∪</mo> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mn>1</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>2</mn> <mo stretchy="false">]</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mo>,</mo> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle displaystyle="false"
   scriptlevel="0"> <mfrac> <mrow> <mn>2</mn> <msup> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mn>2</mn> </mrow> </msup> <mo>−</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo>
   <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </mfrac> </mstyle> </mrow> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f:[0,1)\cup (1,2]\to \mathbb {R} ,f(x)={\tfrac
   {2x^{2}-x-1}{x-1}}.}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:[0,1)\cup (1,2]\to \mathbb {R} ,f(x)={\tfrac
   {2x^{2}-x-1}{x-1}}.}
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mn>3</mn> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   1}f(x)=3}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 1}f(x)=3} because for every ε > 0, we can
   take δ = ε/2, so that for all real x ≠ 1, if 0 < |x − 1| < δ, then
   |f(x) − 3| < ε. Note that here f(1) is undefined.

   In fact, a limit can exist in
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">{</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>a</mi>
   <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>⊂</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mspace width="1em" /> <mi>p</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>a</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mtext> and </mtext> </mrow> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>a</mi>
   <mo>,</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>∪</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>⊂</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">}</mo> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \{x\in \mathbb {R}
   \,|\,\exists (a,b)\subset \mathbb {R} \quad p\in (a,b){\text{ and
   }}(a,p)\cup (p,b)\subset S\},}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \{x\in \mathbb {R} \,|\,\exists (a,b)\subset \mathbb {R}
   \quad p\in (a,b){\text{ and }}(a,p)\cup (p,b)\subset S\},} which equals
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>int</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo>∪</mo> <mi>iso</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <msup> <mi>S</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>c</mi> </mrow> </msup> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \operatorname {int} S\cup
   \operatorname {iso} S^{c},}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \operatorname {int} S\cup \operatorname {iso} S^{c},}
   where int S is the [295]interior of S, and iso S^c are the
   [296]isolated points of the complement of S. In our previous example
   where
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>S</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>∪</mo> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mn>1</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>2</mn> <mo stretchy="false">]</mo> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle S=[0,1)\cup
   (1,2],}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle S=[0,1)\cup (1,2],}
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>int</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo>=</mo> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>1</mn>
   <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>∪</mo> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mn>1</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>2</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \operatorname {int}
   S=(0,1)\cup (1,2),}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \operatorname {int} S=(0,1)\cup (1,2),}
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>iso</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <msup>
   <mi>S</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>c</mi> </mrow> </msup>
   <mo>=</mo> <mo fence="false" stretchy="false">{</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo
   fence="false" stretchy="false">}</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \operatorname
   {iso} S^{c}=\{1\}.}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \operatorname {iso} S^{c}=\{1\}.} We see, specifically,
   this definition of limit allows a limit to exist at 1, but not 0 or 2.

   The letters ε and δ can be understood as "error" and "distance". In
   fact, Cauchy used ε as an abbreviation for "error" in some of his
   work,^[297][2] though in his definition of continuity, he used an
   infinitesimal
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>α</mi> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \alpha
   }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \alpha } rather than either ε or δ (see [298]Cours
   d'Analyse). In these terms, the error (ε) in the measurement of the
   value at the limit can be made as small as desired, by reducing the
   distance (δ) to the limit point. As discussed below, this definition
   also works for functions in a more general context. The idea that δ and
   ε represent distances helps suggest these generalizations.

Existence and one-sided limits

   [[299]edit]

   Main article: [300]One-sided limit

   [301][220px-Upper_semi.svg.png] The limit as
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msubsup> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mn>0</mn> </mrow> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>+</mo> </mrow> </msubsup> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle x\to
   x_{0}^{+}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle x\to x_{0}^{+}} differs from that as
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msubsup> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mn>0</mn> </mrow> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> </mrow> </msubsup> <mo>.</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle x\to
   x_{0}^{-}.}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle x\to x_{0}^{-}.} Therefore, the limit as x → x[0] does
   not exist.

   Alternatively, x may approach p from above (right) or below (left), in
   which case the limits may be written as

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mi>p</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>+</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   p^{+}}f(x)=L}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p^{+}}f(x)=L}

   or

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mi>p</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   p^{-}}f(x)=L}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p^{-}}f(x)=L}
   [302][220px-Undefined_limit_examples.png] The first three functions
   have points for which the limit does not exist, while the function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>sin</mi>
   <mo>⁡</mo> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac> </mrow> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f(x)={\frac {\sin(x)}{x}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\frac {\sin(x)}{x}}} is not defined at
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>x</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mn>0</mn>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle x=0}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle x=0} , but its limit does exist.

   respectively. If these limits exist at p and are equal there, then this
   can be referred to as the limit of f(x) at p.^[303][7] If the one-sided
   limits exist at p, but are unequal, then there is no limit at p (i.e.,
   the limit at p does not exist). If either one-sided limit does not
   exist at p, then the limit at p also does not exist.

   A formal definition is as follows. The limit of f as x approaches p
   from above is L if:

          For every ε > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that whenever 0 < x −
          p < δ, we have |f(x) − L| < ε.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>a</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn>
   <mo><</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mspace width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in
   (a,b))\,(0<x-p<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall
   x\in (a,b))\,(0<x-p<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   The limit of f as x approaches p from below is L if:

          For every ε > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that whenever 0 < p −
          x < δ, we have |f(x) − L| < ε.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>a</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>b</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn>
   <mo><</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mspace width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in
   (a,b))\,(0<p-x<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall
   x\in (a,b))\,(0<p-x<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   If the limit does not exist, then the [304]oscillation of f at p is
   non-zero.

More general definition using limit points and subsets

   [[305]edit]

   Further information: [306]Limit point

   Limits can also be defined by approaching from subsets of the domain.

   In general:^[307][8] Let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb
   {R} }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb {R} } be a real-valued function defined
   on some
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>S</mi> <mo>⊆</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle S\subseteq \mathbb {R}
   .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle S\subseteq \mathbb {R} .} Let p be a [308]limit point of
   some
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>T</mi> <mo>⊂</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle T\subset S}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle T\subset S} —that is, p is the limit of some sequence of
   elements of T distinct from p. Then we say the limit of f, as x
   approaches p from values in T, is L, written
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac
   linethickness="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>p</mi> </mrow> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>T</mi> </mrow>
   </mfrac> </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim
   _{{x\to p} \atop {x\in T}}f(x)=L}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{{x\to p} \atop {x\in T}}f(x)=L} if the following
   holds:

   For every ε > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that for all x ∈ T, 0 < |x −
   p| < δ implies that |f(x) − L| < ε.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>T</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo><</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in T)\,(0<|x-p|<\delta
   \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall
   x\in T)\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   Note, T can be any subset of S, the domain of f. And the limit might
   depend on the selection of T. This generalization includes as special
   cases limits on an interval, as well as left-handed limits of
   real-valued functions (e.g., by taking T to be an open interval of the
   form (–∞, a)), and right-handed limits (e.g., by taking T to be an open
   interval of the form (a, ∞)). It also extends the notion of one-sided
   limits to the included endpoints of (half-)closed intervals, so the
   [309]square root function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <msqrt> <mi>x</mi> </msqrt>
   </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f(x)={\sqrt
   {x}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\sqrt {x}}} can have limit 0 as x approaches 0
   from above:
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac
   linethickness="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mrow> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> </mrow>
   </mfrac> </mrow> </munder> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <msqrt>
   <mi>x</mi> </msqrt> </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{{x\to 0}
   \atop {x\in [0,\infty )}}{\sqrt {x}}=0}</annotation> </semantics>
   :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{{x\to 0} \atop {x\in [0,\infty )}}{\sqrt {x}}=0}
   since for every ε > 0, we may take δ = ε such that for all x ≥ 0, if 0
   < |x − 0| < δ, then |f(x) − 0| < ε.

   This definition allows a limit to be defined at limit points of the
   domain S, if a suitable subset T which has the same limit point is
   chosen.

   Notably, the previous two-sided definition works on
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>int</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo>∪</mo> <mi>iso</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <msup> <mi>S</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>c</mi> </mrow> </msup> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \operatorname {int} S\cup
   \operatorname {iso} S^{c},}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \operatorname {int} S\cup \operatorname {iso} S^{c},}
   which is a subset of the limit points of S.

   For example, let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>S</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>1</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>∪</mo> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mn>1</mn> <mo>,</mo> <mn>2</mn> <mo stretchy="false">]</mo> <mo>.</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle S=[0,1)\cup
   (1,2].}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle S=[0,1)\cup (1,2].} The previous two-sided definition
   would work at
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mn>1</mn> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>iso</mi>
   <mo>⁡</mo> <msup> <mi>S</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>c</mi>
   </mrow> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mo fence="false" stretchy="false">{</mo>
   <mn>1</mn> <mo fence="false" stretchy="false">}</mo> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle 1\in \operatorname {iso}
   S^{c}=\{1\},}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle 1\in \operatorname {iso} S^{c}=\{1\},} but it wouldn't
   work at 0 or 2, which are limit points of S.

Deleted versus non-deleted limits

   [[310]edit]

   The definition of limit given here does not depend on how (or whether)
   f is defined at p. Bartle^[311][9] refers to this as a deleted limit,
   because it excludes the value of f at p. The corresponding non-deleted
   limit does depend on the value of f at p, if p is in the domain of f.
   Let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb
   {R} }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb {R} } be a real-valued function. The
   non-deleted limit of f, as x approaches p, is L if

   For every ε > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that for all x ∈ S, |x − p|
   < δ implies |f(x) − L| < ε.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>δ</mi> <mspace width="thickmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo> <mi>ε</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon
   >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in S)\,(|x-p|<\delta \implies
   |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall
   x\in S)\,(|x-p|<\delta \implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   The definition is the same, except that the neighborhood |x − p| < δ
   now includes the point p, in contrast to the [312]deleted neighborhood
   0 < |x − p| < δ. This makes the definition of a non-deleted limit less
   general. One of the advantages of working with non-deleted limits is
   that they allow to state the [313]theorem about limits of compositions
   without any constraints on the functions (other than the existence of
   their non-deleted limits).^[314][10]

   Bartle^[315][9] notes that although by "limit" some authors do mean
   this non-deleted limit, deleted limits are the most popular.^[316][11]

Examples

   [[317]edit]

Non-existence of one-sided limit(s)

   [[318]edit]

   [319][220px-Discontinuity_essential.svg.png] Function without a limit
   at an [320]essential discontinuity

   The function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mrow> <mo>{</mo> <mtable
   columnalign="left left" rowspacing=".2em 0.4em 0.2em"
   columnspacing="1em" displaystyle="false"> <mtr> <mtd> <mi>sin</mi>
   <mo>⁡</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>5</mn> <mrow>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </mfrac> </mrow> </mtd> <mtd>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> for </mtext> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo><</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> <mtd>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> for </mtext> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>=</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mrow> <mn>10</mn>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mn>10</mn> </mrow> </mfrac> </mrow> </mtd> <mtd>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> for </mtext> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> <mo fence="true"
   stretchy="true" symmetric="true"></mo> </mrow> </mrow> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f(x)={\begin{cases}\sin {\frac {5}{x-1}}&{\text{ for }}x<1\\0&{\text{
   for }}x=1\\[2pt]{\frac {1}{10x-10}}&{\text{ for
   }}x>1\end{cases}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}\sin {\frac {5}{x-1}}&{\text{ for
   }}x<1\\0&{\text{ for }}x=1\\[2pt]{\frac {1}{10x-10}}&{\text{ for
   }}x>1\end{cases}}} has no limit at x[0] = 1 (the left-hand limit does
   not exist due to the oscillatory nature of the sine function, and the
   right-hand limit does not exist due to the asymptotic behaviour of the
   reciprocal function, see picture), but has a limit at every other
   x-coordinate.

   The function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mrow> <mo>{</mo> <mtable
   columnalign="left left" rowspacing=".2em" columnspacing="1em"
   displaystyle="false"> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> rational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd>
   </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> irrational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd>
   </mtr> </mtable> <mo fence="true" stretchy="true"
   symmetric="true"></mo> </mrow> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f(x)={\begin{cases}1&x{\text{ rational }}\\0&x{\text{ irrational
   }}\end{cases}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}1&x{\text{ rational }}\\0&x{\text{
   irrational }}\end{cases}}} (a.k.a., the [321]Dirichlet function) has no
   limit at any x-coordinate.

Non-equality of one-sided limits

   [[322]edit]

   The function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mrow> <mo>{</mo> <mtable
   columnalign="left left" rowspacing=".2em" columnspacing="1em"
   displaystyle="false"> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>1</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> for </mtext> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo><</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>2</mn> </mtd> <mtd>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> for </mtext> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>≥</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> <mo fence="true"
   stretchy="true" symmetric="true"></mo> </mrow> </mrow> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f(x)={\begin{cases}1&{\text{ for }}x<0\\2&{\text{ for }}x\geq
   0\end{cases}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}1&{\text{ for }}x<0\\2&{\text{ for
   }}x\geq 0\end{cases}}} has a limit at every non-zero x-coordinate (the
   limit equals 1 for negative x and equals 2 for positive x). The limit
   at x = 0 does not exist (the left-hand limit equals 1, whereas the
   right-hand limit equals 2).

Limits at only one point

   [[323]edit]

   The functions
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mrow> <mo>{</mo> <mtable
   columnalign="left left" rowspacing=".2em" columnspacing="1em"
   displaystyle="false"> <mtr> <mtd> <mi>x</mi> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> rational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd>
   </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mtext> irrational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd>
   </mtr> </mtable> <mo fence="true" stretchy="true"
   symmetric="true"></mo> </mrow> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f(x)={\begin{cases}x&x{\text{ rational }}\\0&x{\text{ irrational
   }}\end{cases}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}x&x{\text{ rational }}\\0&x{\text{
   irrational }}\end{cases}}} and
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mrow> <mo>{</mo> <mtable
   columnalign="left left" rowspacing=".2em" columnspacing="1em"
   displaystyle="false"> <mtr> <mtd> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> </mtd>
   <mtd> <mi>x</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mtext> rational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd> <mn>0</mn>
   </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mtext> irrational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> <mo
   fence="true" stretchy="true" symmetric="true"></mo> </mrow> </mrow>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
   f(x)={\begin{cases}|x|&x{\text{ rational }}\\0&x{\text{ irrational
   }}\end{cases}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}|x|&x{\text{ rational }}\\0&x{\text{
   irrational }}\end{cases}}} both have a limit at x = 0 and it equals 0.

Limits at countably many points

   [[324]edit]

   The function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mrow> <mo>{</mo> <mtable
   columnalign="left left" rowspacing=".2em" columnspacing="1em"
   displaystyle="false"> <mtr> <mtd> <mi>sin</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <mi>x</mi>
   </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mtext> irrational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd> </mtr> <mtr> <mtd>
   <mn>1</mn> </mtd> <mtd> <mi>x</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mtext> rational </mtext> </mrow> </mtd> </mtr> </mtable> <mo
   fence="true" stretchy="true" symmetric="true"></mo> </mrow> </mrow>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}\sin
   x&x{\text{ irrational }}\\1&x{\text{ rational
   }}\end{cases}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\begin{cases}\sin x&x{\text{ irrational
   }}\\1&x{\text{ rational }}\end{cases}}} has a limit at any x-coordinate
   of the form
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mstyle displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"> <mfrac> <mi>π</mi>
   <mn>2</mn> </mfrac> </mstyle> </mrow> <mo>+</mo> <mn>2</mn> <mi>n</mi>
   <mi>π</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle {\tfrac {\pi }{2}}+2n\pi
   ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle {\tfrac {\pi }{2}}+2n\pi ,} where n is any integer.

Limits involving infinity

   [[325]edit]

Limits at infinity

   [[326]edit]

   [327][300px-Limit_Infinity_SVG.svg.png] The limit of this function at
   infinity exists

   Let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb
   {R} }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb {R} } be a function defined on
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>S</mi> <mo>⊆</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle S\subseteq \mathbb {R}
   .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle S\subseteq \mathbb {R} .} The limit of f as x approaches
   infinity is L, denoted

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> </mrow>
   </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim
   _{x\to \infty }f(x)=L,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to \infty }f(x)=L,}

   means that:

   For every ε > 0, there exists a c > 0 such that whenever +x > c, we
   have |f(x) − L| < ε.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>c</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>></mo> <mi>c</mi> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists c>0)\,(\forall x\in S)\,(x>c\implies
   |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists c>0)\,(\forall x\in
   S)\,(x>c\implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   Similarly, the limit of f as x approaches minus infinity is L, denoted

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim
   _{x\to -\infty }f(x)=L,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to -\infty }f(x)=L,}

   means that:

   For every ε > 0, there exists a c > 0 such that whenever x < −c, we
   have |f(x) − L| < ε.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>ε</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>c</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo><</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>c</mi>
   <mspace width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>L</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo>
   <mi>ε</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall
   \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists c>0)\,(\forall x\in S)\,(x<-c\implies
   |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall \varepsilon >0)\,(\exists c>0)\,(\forall x\in
   S)\,(x<-c\implies |f(x)-L|<\varepsilon ).}

   For example,
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> </mrow>
   </munder> <mrow> <mo>(</mo> <mrow> <mo>−</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mrow> <mn>3</mn> <mi>sin</mi>
   <mo>⁡</mo> <mi>x</mi> </mrow> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac> </mrow> <mo>+</mo>
   <mn>4</mn> </mrow> <mo>)</mo> </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mn>4</mn> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim
   _{x\to \infty }\left(-{\frac {3\sin x}{x}}+4\right)=4}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to \infty }\left(-{\frac {3\sin
   x}{x}}+4\right)=4} because for every ε > 0, we can take c = 3/ε such
   that for all real x, if x > c, then |f(x) − 4| < ε.

   Another example is that
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   </mrow> </munder> <msup> <mi>e</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mi>x</mi> </mrow> </msup> <mo>=</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   -\infty }e^{x}=0}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to -\infty }e^{x}=0} because for every ε > 0,
   we can take c = max{1, −ln(ε)} such that for all real x, if x < −c,
   then |f(x) − 0| < ε.

Infinite limits

   [[328]edit]

   For a function whose values grow without bound, the function diverges
   and the usual limit does not exist. However, in this case one may
   introduce limits with infinite values.

   Let
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb
   {R} }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:S\to \mathbb {R} } be a function defined on
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>S</mi> <mo>⊆</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle S\subseteq \mathbb {R}
   .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle S\subseteq \mathbb {R} .} The statement the limit of f
   as x approaches p is infinity, denoted

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>p</mi> </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   p}f(x)=\infty ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p}f(x)=\infty ,}

   means that:

   For every N > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that whenever 0 < |x − p| <
   δ, we have f(x) > N.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>N</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo><</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>></mo> <mi>N</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall N>0)\,(\exists
   \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in S)\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies
   f(x)>N).}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall N>0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in
   S)\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies f(x)>N).}

   The statement the limit of f as x approaches p is minus infinity,
   denoted

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>p</mi> </mrow> </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p}f(x)=-\infty
   ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p}f(x)=-\infty ,}

   means that:

   For every N > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that whenever 0 < |x − p| <
   δ, we have f(x) < −N.

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>N</mi> <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∃</mi> <mi>δ</mi>
   <mo>></mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace
   width="thinmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∀</mi> <mi>x</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mi>S</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mspace width="thinmathspace" /> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mn>0</mn> <mo><</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mo>−</mo> <mi>p</mi> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mo><</mo> <mi>δ</mi> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo> <mspace
   width="thickmathspace" /> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo><</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi>N</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle (\forall N>0)\,(\exists
   \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in S)\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies
   f(x)<-N).}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle (\forall N>0)\,(\exists \delta >0)\,(\forall x\in
   S)\,(0<|x-p|<\delta \implies f(x)<-N).}

   For example,
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </munder> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mrow> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>−</mo> <mn>1</mn> <msup> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mn>2</mn>
   </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </mfrac> </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 1}{\frac
   {1}{(x-1)^{2}}}=\infty }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 1}{\frac {1}{(x-1)^{2}}}=\infty } because
   for every N > 0, we can take
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>δ</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0">
   <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mrow> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <msqrt>
   <mi>N</mi> </msqrt> </mrow> <mi>δ</mi> </mrow> </mfrac> </mstyle>
   </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="false" scriptlevel="0"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <msqrt>
   <mi>N</mi> </msqrt> </mfrac> </mstyle> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\textstyle \delta ={\tfrac
   {1}{{\sqrt {N}}\delta }}={\tfrac {1}{\sqrt {N}}}}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\textstyle \delta ={\tfrac {1}{{\sqrt {N}}\delta }}={\tfrac {1}{\sqrt
   {N}}}} such that for all real x > 0, if 0 < x − 1 < δ, then f(x) > N.

   These ideas can be used together to produce definitions for different
   combinations, such as

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> </mrow>
   </munder> <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to \infty
   }f(x)=\infty ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to \infty }f(x)=\infty ,} or
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mi>p</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>+</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mi>f</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo> <mo>=</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   p^{+}}f(x)=-\infty .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to p^{+}}f(x)=-\infty .}

   For example,
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mn>0</mn> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>+</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mi>ln</mi> <mo>⁡</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>=</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 0^{+}}\ln
   x=-\infty }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 0^{+}}\ln x=-\infty } because for every N >
   0, we can take δ = e^−N such that for all real x > 0, if 0 < x − 0 < δ,
   then f(x) < −N.

   Limits involving infinity are connected with the concept of
   [329]asymptotes.

   These notions of a limit attempt to provide a metric space
   interpretation to limits at infinity. In fact, they are consistent with
   the topological space definition of limit if
     * a neighborhood of −∞ is defined to contain an [330]interval [−∞, c)
       for some ⁠
       [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
       displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>c</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mrow
       class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi>
       </mrow> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle c\in \mathbb {R}
       ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
       {\displaystyle c\in \mathbb {R} ,} ⁠
     * a neighborhood of ∞ is defined to contain an interval (c, ∞] where
       ⁠
       [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
       displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>c</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mrow
       class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi>
       </mrow> <mo>,</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle c\in \mathbb {R}
       ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
       {\displaystyle c\in \mathbb {R} ,} ⁠ and
     * a neighborhood of ⁠
       [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
       displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>a</mi> <mo>∈</mo> <mrow
       class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi>
       </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle a\in \mathbb {R}
       }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
       {\displaystyle a\in \mathbb {R} } ⁠ is defined in the normal way
       metric space ⁠
       [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
       displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
       <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle>
       </mrow> <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle
       \mathbb {R} .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
       {\displaystyle \mathbb {R} .} ⁠

   In this case, ⁠
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD">
   <mover> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mo accent="false">¯</mo>
   </mover> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle {\overline {\mathbb {R}
   }}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle {\overline {\mathbb {R} }}} ⁠ is a topological space and
   any function of the form
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>X</mi>
   <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mi>Y</mi> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:X\to Y}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:X\to Y} with
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>X</mi> <mo>,</mo> <mi>Y</mi>
   <mo>⊆</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mover> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   <mo accent="false">¯</mo> </mover> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle X,Y\subseteq
   {\overline {\mathbb {R} }}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle X,Y\subseteq {\overline {\mathbb {R} }}} is subject to
   the topological definition of a limit. Note that with this topological
   definition, it is easy to define infinite limits at finite points,
   which have not been defined above in the metric sense.

Alternative notation

   [[331]edit]

   Many authors^[332][12] allow for the [333]projectively extended real
   line to be used as a way to include infinite values as well as
   [334]extended real line. With this notation, the extended real line is
   given as ⁠
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mo>∪</mo> <mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">{</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   <mo>,</mo> <mo>+</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">}</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \mathbb {R} \cup \{-\infty
   ,+\infty \}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \mathbb {R} \cup \{-\infty ,+\infty \}} ⁠ and the
   projectively extended real line is ⁠
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mo>∪</mo> <mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">{</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo
   fence="false" stretchy="false">}</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \mathbb {R} \cup \{\infty
   \}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \mathbb {R} \cup \{\infty \}} ⁠ where a neighborhood of
   ∞ is a set of the form
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">{</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow> <mi>x</mi>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo stretchy="false">|</mo> </mrow>
   <mo>></mo> <mi>c</mi> <mo fence="false" stretchy="false">}</mo>
   <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \{x:|x|>c\}.}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \{x:|x|>c\}.} The advantage is that one only needs three
   definitions for limits (left, right, and central) to cover all the
   cases. As presented above, for a completely rigorous account, we would
   need to consider 15 separate cases for each combination of infinities
   (five directions: −∞, left, central, right, and +∞; three bounds: −∞,
   finite, or +∞). There are also noteworthy pitfalls. For example, when
   working with the extended real line,
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <msup> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </msup>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle x^{-1}}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle x^{-1}} does not possess a central limit (which is
   normal):

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mn>0</mn> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>+</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac>
   </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mo>+</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   <mo>,</mo> <mspace width="1em" /> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mn>0</mn> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac>
   </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 0^{+}}{1 \over
   x}=+\infty ,\quad \lim _{x\to 0^{-}}{1 \over x}=-\infty .}</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 0^{+}}{1 \over x}=+\infty ,\quad \lim _{x\to
   0^{-}}{1 \over x}=-\infty .}

   In contrast, when working with the projective real line, infinities
   (much like 0) are unsigned, so, the central limit does exist in that
   context:

   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mn>0</mn> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>+</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac>
   </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mn>0</mn> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac>
   </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mrow> </munder> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mfrac> <mn>1</mn> <mi>x</mi> </mfrac> </mrow>
   <mo>=</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo>.</mo> </mstyle> </mrow>
   <annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   0^{+}}{1 \over x}=\lim _{x\to 0^{-}}{1 \over x}=\lim _{x\to 0}{1 \over
   x}=\infty .}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 0^{+}}{1 \over x}=\lim _{x\to 0^{-}}{1 \over
   x}=\lim _{x\to 0}{1 \over x}=\infty .}

   In fact there are a plethora of conflicting formal systems in use. In
   certain applications of [335]numerical differentiation and integration,
   it is, for example, convenient to have [336]signed zeroes. A simple
   reason has to do with the converse of
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <msup> <mn>0</mn> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> </mrow> </msup> </mrow> </munder>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <msup> <mi>x</mi> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </msup> </mrow>
   <mo>=</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to
   0^{-}}{x^{-1}}=-\infty ,}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to 0^{-}}{x^{-1}}=-\infty ,} namely, it is
   convenient for
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <munder> <mo movablelimits="true"
   form="prefix">lim</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi>x</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi>
   </mrow> </munder> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <msup> <mi>x</mi>
   <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mo>−</mo> <mn>1</mn> </mrow> </msup>
   </mrow> <mo>=</mo> <mo>−</mo> <mn>0</mn> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle \lim _{x\to -\infty
   }{x^{-1}}=-0}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle \lim _{x\to -\infty }{x^{-1}}=-0} to be considered true.
   Such zeroes can be seen as an approximation to [337]infinitesimals.

Limits at infinity for rational functions

   [[338]edit]

   [339][300px-Tamasol_SVG.svg.png] Horizontal asymptote about y = 4

   There are three basic rules for evaluating limits at infinity for a
   [340]rational function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo>
   <mo>=</mo> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle displaystyle="false"
   scriptlevel="0"> <mfrac> <mrow> <mi>p</mi> <mo stretchy="false">(</mo>
   <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> </mrow> <mrow> <mi>q</mi> <mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo> <mi>x</mi> <mo stretchy="false">)</mo> </mrow>
   </mfrac> </mstyle> </mrow> </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f(x)={\tfrac
   {p(x)}{q(x)}}}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f(x)={\tfrac {p(x)}{q(x)}}} (where p and q are
   polynomials):
     * If the [341]degree of p is greater than the degree of q, then the
       limit is positive or negative infinity depending on the signs of
       the leading coefficients;
     * If the degree of p and q are equal, the limit is the leading
       coefficient of p divided by the leading coefficient of q;
     * If the degree of p is less than the degree of q, the limit is 0.

   If the limit at infinity exists, it represents a horizontal asymptote
   at y = L. Polynomials do not have horizontal asymptotes; such
   asymptotes may however occur with rational functions.

Functions of more than one variable

   [[342]edit]

Ordinary limits

   [[343]edit]

   By noting that |x − p| represents a [344]distance, the definition of a
   limit can be extended to functions of more than one variable. In the
   case of a function
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>f</mi> <mo>:</mo> <mi>S</mi>
   <mo>×</mo> <mi>T</mi> <mo stretchy="false">→</mo> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle f:S\times T\to \mathbb {R}
   }</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle f:S\times T\to \mathbb {R} } defined on
   [MATH: <semantics> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mstyle
   displaystyle="true" scriptlevel="0"> <mi>S</mi> <mo>×</mo> <mi>T</mi>
   <mo>⊆</mo> <msup> <mrow class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mi
   mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi> </mrow> <mrow
   class="MJX-TeXAtom-ORD"> <mn>2</mn> </mrow> </msup> <mo>,</mo>
   </mstyle> </mrow> <annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\displaystyle S\times T\subseteq \mathbb
   {R} ^{2},}</annotation> </semantics> :MATH]
   {\displaystyle S\times T\subseteq \mathbb {R} ^{2},} we defined the
   limit as follows: the limit of f as (x, y) approaches (p, q) is L,
